Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is my head just feels… cloudy. Like at work, I’ll be looking at a board packet or some vendor email, and I just sit there staring at it. I used to make those little decisions pretty quick, you know, which repair quote to send along or what needs an answer first.

And now even simple things feel harder to sort through. I’ll start reading, then I’m thinking about the last association meeting, or whether I forgot to call somebody back, and then I’ve lost the point of the email completely. It’s frustrating. That’s really the main thing that made me think I should talk to someone.

So I’m not sure if that’s “down” or just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worrying a lot, exactly. I mean, there’s always something to think about with the properties—somebody’s roof, or the landscaper didn’t show, or a board member wants numbers by Thursday.\n\nBut when there isn’t something right in front of me… I don’t know. My mind still kind of wanders around to things, like did I send that notice, or I’ll remember a tenant from years ago who used to call about every little noise. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… my brain not staying put.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That was such a long time ago. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.

Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.

Like at work, I’ll be looking at a board packet or some vendor email, and I just sit there staring at it. I used to make those little decisions pretty quick, you know, which repair quote to send along or what needs an answer first.\n\nAnd now even simple things feel harder to sort through. I’ll start reading, then I’m thinking about the last association meeting, or whether I forgot to call somebody back, and then I’ve lost the point of the email completely. It’s frustrating. That’s really the main thing that made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I’m just kind of… flat, I guess, but then everybody has those, right? Especially when the work piles up.\n\nThe other day I was trying to sort through some papers for one of the properties, and I started thinking about this old filing cabinet we used to have years ago—funny what you remember—and then I realized I hadn’t really answered the email I opened. So I’m not sure if that’s “down” or just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worrying a lot, exactly. I mean, there’s always something to think about with the properties—somebody’s roof, or the landscaper didn’t show, or a board member wants numbers by Thursday.\n\nBut when there isn’t something right in front of me… I don’t know. My mind still kind of wanders around to things, like did I send that notice, or I’ll remember a tenant from years ago who used to call about every little noise. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… my brain not staying put.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That was such a long time ago. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual. Some days I’m just sitting there after work and I think, “Well, I should get up and do something,” and then I start thinking about the tenants and this one lady who keeps calling about the gate code even though we changed it months ago—\n\nSorry. Energy-wise, I guess I haven’t really been paying close attention. I wouldn’t say I’m completely wiped out or anything. It’s kind of hard for me to tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… concentration, that one, yes. That’s kind of what made me think I should come in.

My head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet, or some vendor email about a repair, and I just stare at it. I used to be able to decide those things pretty quick—call this person, approve that, put it on the agenda—but lately even simple things feel like they get stuck.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is my head just feels… cloudy. Like at work, I’ll be looking at a board packet or some vendor email, and I just sit there staring at it. I used to make those little decisions pretty quick, you know, which repair quote to send along or what needs an answer first.\n\nAnd now even simple things feel harder to sort through. I’ll start reading, then I’m thinking about the last association meeting, or whether I forgot to call somebody back, and then I’ve lost the point of the email completely. It’s frustrating. That’s really the main thing that made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I’m just kind of… flat, I guess, but then everybody has those, right? Especially when the work piles up.\n\nThe other day I was trying to sort through some papers for one of the properties, and I started thinking about this old filing cabinet we used to have years ago—funny what you remember—and then I realized I hadn’t really answered the email I opened. So I’m not sure if that’s “down” or just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worrying a lot, exactly. I mean, there’s always something to think about with the properties—somebody’s roof, or the landscaper didn’t show, or a board member wants numbers by Thursday.\n\nBut when there isn’t something right in front of me… I don’t know. My mind still kind of wanders around to things, like did I send that notice, or I’ll remember a tenant from years ago who used to call about every little noise. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… my brain not staying put.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That was such a long time ago. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual. Some days I’m just sitting there after work and I think, “Well, I should get up and do something,” and then I start thinking about the tenants and this one lady who keeps calling about the gate code even though we changed it months ago—\n\nSorry. Energy-wise, I guess I haven’t really been paying close attention. I wouldn’t say I’m completely wiped out or anything. It’s kind of hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had trouble concentrating nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration, that one, yes. That’s kind of what made me think I should come in.\n\nMy head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet, or some vendor email about a repair, and I just stare at it. I used to be able to decide those things pretty quick—call this person, approve that, put it on the agenda—but lately even simple things feel like they get stuck.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about the building, or who complained last month, or whether I forgot to tell someone something, and I’m not really reading the thing in front of me anymore. So… yes, I guess I have had trouble concentrating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down and eat like I should. I’ll have coffee, maybe a few bites of something, and then somehow that carries me most of the day.

Then at night I’ll pick at things—tortillas, or something sweet if it’s there—but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just standing in the kitchen looking around, you know? I mean, I’ve had days where I’m just kind of… flat, I guess, but then everybody has those, right? Especially when the work piles up.\n\nThe other day I was trying to sort through some papers for one of the properties, and I started thinking about this old filing cabinet we used to have years ago—funny what you remember—and then I realized I hadn’t really answered the email I opened. So I’m not sure if that’s “down” or just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worrying a lot, exactly. I mean, there’s always something to think about with the properties—somebody’s roof, or the landscaper didn’t show, or a board member wants numbers by Thursday.\n\nBut when there isn’t something right in front of me… I don’t know. My mind still kind of wanders around to things, like did I send that notice, or I’ll remember a tenant from years ago who used to call about every little noise. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… my brain not staying put.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That was such a long time ago. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual. Some days I’m just sitting there after work and I think, “Well, I should get up and do something,” and then I start thinking about the tenants and this one lady who keeps calling about the gate code even though we changed it months ago—\n\nSorry. Energy-wise, I guess I haven’t really been paying close attention. I wouldn’t say I’m completely wiped out or anything. It’s kind of hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had trouble concentrating nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration, that one, yes. That’s kind of what made me think I should come in.\n\nMy head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet, or some vendor email about a repair, and I just stare at it. I used to be able to decide those things pretty quick—call this person, approve that, put it on the agenda—but lately even simple things feel like they get stuck.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about the building, or who complained last month, or whether I forgot to tell someone something, and I’m not really reading the thing in front of me anymore. So… yes, I guess I have had trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down and eat like I should. I’ll have coffee, maybe a few bites of something, and then somehow that carries me most of the day.\n\nThen at night I’ll pick at things—tortillas, or something sweet if it’s there—but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just standing in the kitchen looking around, you know? Like when the counter has mail on it and I’m moving things from one pile to another… anyway, yes, my eating hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about weight, honestly. I don’t really check the scale much. My eating’s been off, though — like I’ll have coffee and a few bites of something and then somehow that’s the whole day, and later I’m just picking at tortillas or sweets, not even really wanting them.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel a little… I don’t know. But I can’t say for sure I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: A few mornings… yeah, I’ve had this scary thought, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. It kind of comes when I’m first lying there and thinking about all the things waiting for me, and then I’ll hear the trash truck or remember some little thing I was supposed to do, and it shifts—\n\nBut no, I don’t want to hurt myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that. It just scares me that the thought even shows up.\n\nDoctor: Have these changes been going on for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… about two weeks, I’d say. It kind of sharpened up around when my husband retired and was suddenly home all day. Not that he’s doing anything terrible, it’s just—having someone there all the time changes the whole feel of the house, you know?\n\nSince then my energy and confidence have been different, and then this low, cloudy feeling sort of settled in. So yes, at least two weeks, maybe right around that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual. Some days I’m just sitting there after work and I think, “Well, I should get up and do something,” and then I start thinking about the tenants and this one lady who keeps calling about the gate code even though we changed it months ago—\n\nSorry. Energy-wise, I guess I haven’t really been paying close attention. I wouldn’t say I’m completely wiped out or anything. It’s kind of hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had trouble concentrating nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration, that one, yes. That’s kind of what made me think I should come in.\n\nMy head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet, or some vendor email about a repair, and I just stare at it. I used to be able to decide those things pretty quick—call this person, approve that, put it on the agenda—but lately even simple things feel like they get stuck.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about the building, or who complained last month, or whether I forgot to tell someone something, and I’m not really reading the thing in front of me anymore. So… yes, I guess I have had trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down and eat like I should. I’ll have coffee, maybe a few bites of something, and then somehow that carries me most of the day.\n\nThen at night I’ll pick at things—tortillas, or something sweet if it’s there—but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just standing in the kitchen looking around, you know? Like when the counter has mail on it and I’m moving things from one pile to another… anyway, yes, my eating hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about weight, honestly. I don’t really check the scale much. My eating’s been off, though — like I’ll have coffee and a few bites of something and then somehow that’s the whole day, and later I’m just picking at tortillas or sweets, not even really wanting them.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel a little… I don’t know. But I can’t say for sure I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: A few mornings… yeah, I’ve had this scary thought, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. It kind of comes when I’m first lying there and thinking about all the things waiting for me, and then I’ll hear the trash truck or remember some little thing I was supposed to do, and it shifts—\n\nBut no, I don’t want to hurt myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that. It just scares me that the thought even shows up.\n\nDoctor: Have these changes been going on for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… about two weeks, I’d say. It kind of sharpened up around when my husband retired and was suddenly home all day. Not that he’s doing anything terrible, it’s just—having someone there all the time changes the whole feel of the house, you know?\n\nSince then my energy and confidence have been different, and then this low, cloudy feeling sort of settled in. So yes, at least two weeks, maybe right around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these problems started because of your husband retiring and being home all day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s because of that exactly, but the timing fits. Like, it got sharper around when he retired and suddenly he was just… there, all day, in the house. And I love him, I do, but it changes the rhythm of everything.\n\nThat’s when my energy and confidence started feeling off, and then that low, cloudy feeling came in. So I’d say it’s been about two weeks, since that all shifted.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings mostly about your husband being home all day since he retired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “mostly,” exactly. It’s just the thing I noticed around the same time, you know? Him being home changes the day—where I put things, when I make phone calls, even little silly things like whether the kitchen table is clear.\n\nBut I don’t want to make it sound like I blame him. It’s more… that shift made me notice how off I was feeling, I think. I’m not sure if it’s the cause or just part of the whole mess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have these changes been going on for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… about two weeks, I’d say. It kind of sharpened up around when my husband retired and was suddenly home all day. Not that he’s doing anything terrible, it’s just—having someone there all the time changes the whole feel of the house, you know?\n\nSince then my energy and confidence have been different, and then this low, cloudy feeling sort of settled in. So yes, at least two weeks, maybe right around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these problems started because of your husband retiring and being home all day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s because of that exactly, but the timing fits. Like, it got sharper around when he retired and suddenly he was just… there, all day, in the house. And I love him, I do, but it changes the rhythm of everything.\n\nThat’s when my energy and confidence started feeling off, and then that low, cloudy feeling came in. So I’d say it’s been about two weeks, since that all shifted.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings mostly about your husband being home all day since he retired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “mostly,” exactly. It’s just the thing I noticed around the same time, you know? Him being home changes the day—where I put things, when I make phone calls, even little silly things like whether the kitchen table is clear.\n\nBut I don’t want to make it sound like I blame him. It’s more… that shift made me notice how off I was feeling, I think. I’m not sure if it’s the cause or just part of the whole mess.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince your husband retired, have you been feeling tense or on edge?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"At home I move slow like my legs are heavy, but in meetings I catch myself tapping my pen, shifting in the chair, and feeling too keyed up to sit still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, tense… sort of. At home it’s funny, because I feel more slowed down, like my legs are heavy and I’m dragging myself from room to room. But then if I’m in a meeting, I notice I’m tapping my pen or shifting around in the chair, like I can’t quite settle.

And then I get embarrassed because people can probably see it, you know? I used to tell the board members not to fidget during budget talks, and now here I am clicking a pen like a kid in class. So yes, I guess I do feel keyed up sometimes, especially when I’m supposed to sit still.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me to come in is my head just feels… cloudy. Like at work, I’ll be looking at a board packet or some vendor email, and I just sit there staring at it. I used to make those little decisions pretty quick, you know, which repair quote to send along or what needs an answer first.\n\nAnd now even simple things feel harder to sort through. I’ll start reading, then I’m thinking about the last association meeting, or whether I forgot to call somebody back, and then I’ve lost the point of the email completely. It’s frustrating. That’s really the main thing that made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had days where I’m just kind of… flat, I guess, but then everybody has those, right? Especially when the work piles up.\n\nThe other day I was trying to sort through some papers for one of the properties, and I started thinking about this old filing cabinet we used to have years ago—funny what you remember—and then I realized I hadn’t really answered the email I opened. So I’m not sure if that’s “down” or just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say worrying a lot, exactly. I mean, there’s always something to think about with the properties—somebody’s roof, or the landscaper didn’t show, or a board member wants numbers by Thursday.\n\nBut when there isn’t something right in front of me… I don’t know. My mind still kind of wanders around to things, like did I send that notice, or I’ll remember a tenant from years ago who used to call about every little noise. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… my brain not staying put.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That was such a long time ago. I left school pretty early, so it all kind of blurs together for me.\n\nI remember teachers talking more about whether you were quiet or behaved, you know, not so much “focus” like people say now. I was probably daydreamy sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it was more than other kids. My mother used to say I had my head somewhere else, but she said that about everybody if they didn’t answer her quick enough.\n\nDoctor: Did this cloudy, hard-to-focus feeling start after any illness, injury, or new medication?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know that it started after anything like that. What I notice is more… my head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet or one of those vendor emails, and I just sit there staring at it.\n\nAnd it’s silly things, too—things I used to decide quickly, like who to call first or whether the wording on a notice is fine. I’ll get stuck, and then I remember some totally different thing, like an old meeting from years ago, and then I’ve lost the thread. I can’t say it was because of an illness or injury, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying your usual activities less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things if I have to, but the things that are just mine, I haven’t really wanted them.\n\nLike my dance class—I used to like going, even if I wasn’t the best one there. And there are those little galleries I’d wander into sometimes, just to look around. Lately I just don’t feel like it. Even Sunday cooking for my family, which usually makes me feel like myself, I keep thinking, “Oh, maybe not this week.” It’s not like me, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days I’m low, I suppose, but “sad most days” feels hard to pin down.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be sitting there and thinking I should be doing something, then I end up remembering some silly thing from years ago—like a tenant meeting where someone got mad about the parking spaces—and then I forget what mood I was even in. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m crying every day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Um… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Some nights I think it’s fine, and then other times I’m lying there thinking about whether I forgot to call someone back at the office, or I remember some totally unrelated thing like the old carpet color in one of the buildings.\n\nBut I haven’t really been keeping track of it. I couldn’t tell you, like, a number of hours or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: Forgetting conversations or appointments… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but doesn’t everybody do that? Or at work, someone will tell me something about a unit—like the water heater or a parking complaint—and then I’ll remember the story about the last building we managed with the same issue, and I get sidetracked. But appointments… I usually write those down. So no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of low, but then I’ll get distracted thinking about some building issue or something someone said at a meeting, and it’s hard to tell if it’s really “depressed” or just… a mood passing through.\n\nSo I’m not sure. I haven’t been counting the days or anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, a little. Or maybe more than a little. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It got me out of the house.\n\nAnd there are these little galleries I sometimes stop into—nothing fancy, just local art and little things in the windows—and lately I just pass by or don’t even think to go. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. I usually like doing that, you know, it makes me feel like myself, like I’m keeping things together. But lately I just… don’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I’m not sure. I mean, I get tired, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual. Some days I’m just sitting there after work and I think, “Well, I should get up and do something,” and then I start thinking about the tenants and this one lady who keeps calling about the gate code even though we changed it months ago—\n\nSorry. Energy-wise, I guess I haven’t really been paying close attention. I wouldn’t say I’m completely wiped out or anything. It’s kind of hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had trouble concentrating nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… concentration, that one, yes. That’s kind of what made me think I should come in.\n\nMy head feels cloudy. Like I’ll be looking at a board packet, or some vendor email about a repair, and I just stare at it. I used to be able to decide those things pretty quick—call this person, approve that, put it on the agenda—but lately even simple things feel like they get stuck.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about the building, or who complained last month, or whether I forgot to tell someone something, and I’m not really reading the thing in front of me anymore. So… yes, I guess I have had trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of off. I don’t really sit down and eat like I should. I’ll have coffee, maybe a few bites of something, and then somehow that carries me most of the day.\n\nThen at night I’ll pick at things—tortillas, or something sweet if it’s there—but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just standing in the kitchen looking around, you know? Like when the counter has mail on it and I’m moving things from one pile to another… anyway, yes, my eating hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about weight, honestly. I don’t really check the scale much. My eating’s been off, though — like I’ll have coffee and a few bites of something and then somehow that’s the whole day, and later I’m just picking at tortillas or sweets, not even really wanting them.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel a little… I don’t know. But I can’t say for sure I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: A few mornings… yeah, I’ve had this scary thought, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. It kind of comes when I’m first lying there and thinking about all the things waiting for me, and then I’ll hear the trash truck or remember some little thing I was supposed to do, and it shifts—\n\nBut no, I don’t want to hurt myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that. It just scares me that the thought even shows up.\n\nDoctor: Have these changes been going on for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… about two weeks, I’d say. So I’d say it’s been about two weeks, since that all shifted.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings mostly about your husband being home all day since he retired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “mostly,” exactly. It’s just the thing I noticed around the same time, you know? Him being home changes the day—where I put things, when I make phone calls, even little silly things like whether the kitchen table is clear.\n\nBut I don’t want to make it sound like I blame him. It’s more… that shift made me notice how off I was feeling, I think. I’m not sure if it’s the cause or just part of the whole mess.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband retired, have you been feeling tense or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, tense… sort of. At home it’s funny, because I feel more slowed down, like my legs are heavy and I’m dragging myself from room to room. But then if I’m in a meeting, I notice I’m tapping my pen or shifting around in the chair, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nAnd then I get embarrassed because people can probably see it, you know? I used to tell the board members not to fidget during budget talks, and now here I am clicking a pen like a kid in class. So yes, I guess I do feel keyed up sometimes, especially when I’m supposed to sit still."
